44. Final Takeaway
The core concept of fertilization is:
Male gamete+Female gamete→Zygote +    
In flowering plants, this occurs as double fertilization:
Sperm + Egg→Zygote→Embryo     

and:
Sperm + Central cell→Primary endosperm nucleus→Endosperm 
   
The complete agricultural reproductive sequence is:
Pollination→Pollen germination→Pollen tube growth→Double fertilization→Embryo + Endosperm→
Seed formation→Fruit/Grain development         
So, in one sentence:
Fertilization is the critical reproductive event in which male gametes delivered by the
pollen tube fuse with the egg and central cell, producing the zygote and endosperm
that ultimately support formation of the next generation of seed plants.

Seed Formation
## 1. Introduction
Seed formation is the developmental process through which a fertilized ovule develops into a mature
seed.
It begins after successful fertilization and involves coordinated development of:
- The zygote → embryo
- The primary endosperm nucleus → endosperm
- The ovule integuments → seed coat
- The embryo sac and surrounding tissues undergo major structural and physiological changes
- Food reserves accumulate
- The seed acquires maturity, often followed by dormancy and desiccation
The basic relationship is:
Ovule→fertilization + developmentMature Seed        
And the complete reproductive sequence is:
Pollination→Fertilization→Embryo development→Endosperm development→Seed maturation          

2. What Is a Seed?
A seed is the mature reproductive structure of a seed plant that contains a young embryo and is adapted
for protection, dispersal, survival and establishment of a new plant.
A mature seed generally consists of:
Seed coat+Embryo+Storage tissue + +   
The storage tissue may be primarily:
- Endosperm
- Cotyledons
- Other specialized tissues
depending on the crop.

3. Where Does the Seed Come From?
This is one of the most important concepts:
Ovule→Seed   
while:
Ovary→Fruit   
Therefore:
## Flower
│
┌────┴────┐
│
│
Ovary
Ovule
│
│
↓
↓
Fruit
Seed

For example, in a tomato:
- Ovary → tomato fruit
- Ovules → tomato seeds
In wheat:
- Ovary develops into the caryopsis (grain)
- The seed is enclosed within that grain structure.

4. When Does Seed Formation Begin?
Seed formation begins after successful fertilization.
The key event is:
Sperm+Egg→Zygote +    
The zygote becomes the embryo.
At the same time:
Second sperm+central cell→Primary endosperm nucleus +    
The primary endosperm nucleus develops into the endosperm.
Therefore:
Fertilization→ Zygote→EmbryoPrimary endosperm nucleus→Endosperm 
  \\  
  

5. Complete Process of Seed Formation
The simplified process is:
Ovule↓Pollination↓Fertilization↓Zygote + Endosperm initiation↓Embryo development↓Endosperm dev
elopment↓Seed coat development↓Reserve accumulation↓Maturation↓Drying/desiccation↓Mature seed
  & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ &     

6. Major Components Formed During Seed
Development
After fertilization, three major developmental pathways occur simultaneously.

## 1. Zygote
Zygote→Embryo   

2. Primary endosperm nucleus
Primary endosperm nucleus→Endosperm 
  

## 3. Integuments
Integuments→Seed coat   
Therefore:
Seed=Embryo+Storage tissue+Protective covering  =   +
  +    

7. Embryo Formation
The embryo is the young plant inside the developing seed.
It originates from the zygote.
Zygote→Embryo    
The zygote undergoes:
- Cell division
- Cell enlargement
- Cell differentiation
- Tissue organization
Eventually, the embryo develops the basic organization of the future plant.

8. Stages of Embryo Development
In a generalized eudicot embryo, development can be described through stages such as:
Zygote→Proembryo→Globular→Heart→Torpedo→Mature embryo        
These names describe characteristic shapes during development.

Zygote
The first cell after fertilization.

Proembryo
Early multicellular embryo.

Globular stage
The embryo becomes approximately spherical.

Heart stage
Cotyledon primordia become visible, giving the embryo a heart-like shape.

Torpedo stage
The embryo elongates.

Mature embryo
The embryo develops recognizable:
- Cotyledons
- Shoot apex
- Root apex
- Hypocotyl
- Epicotyl
These stages are particularly useful for understanding eudicot embryogenesis; monocot embryo
development has a different organization.

9. Monocot Embryo Development
Monocot embryos are structurally different from typical eudicot embryos.
In cereal crops such as:
- Rice
- Wheat
- Maize
the embryo has specialized structures.
A major embryonic structure is the:

Scutellum
It is the single cotyledon of the grass embryo.

Other specialized structures include:
- Coleoptile
- Coleorhiza
- Plumule
- Radicle

## 10. Coleoptile
The coleoptile is a protective sheath surrounding the young shoot in grasses.
It protects the developing plumule as it emerges through the soil.
Therefore:
Coleoptile→Protection of young shoot   
It is especially important in cereal seedlings.

## 11. Coleorhiza
The coleorhiza is a protective sheath surrounding the embryonic root in grasses.
Therefore:
Coleorhiza→Protection of embryonic root   

12. Endosperm Formation
The endosperm is a nutritive tissue that develops after the second fertilization event.
The sequence is:
Second sperm+Central cell→Primary endosperm nucleus→Endosperm  +
     
Endosperm supplies nutrients to the developing embryo.
It can store:
- Starch

- Protein
- Lipids
- Minerals and other compounds
depending on the crop.

13. Types of Endosperm Development
Endosperm development is commonly classified into three broad types:

Nuclear
Nuclear divisions occur initially without immediate cell-wall formation.

Cellular
Cell-wall formation follows each division.

Helobial
An intermediate pattern involving an initial division that establishes two compartments, followed by
different developmental patterns.
These classifications are important in plant embryology but are less critical for basic crop production.

14. Seed Coat Formation
The seed coat develops mainly from the integuments surrounding the ovule.
Ovule integuments→Seed coat    
The seed coat protects the developing embryo and mature seed.
Its functions include:
- Mechanical protection
- Protection from pathogens
- Regulation of water uptake
- Regulation of gas exchange
- Contribution to dormancy
- Protection during storage

15. Testa and Tegmen
In many seeds, the seed coat can have:

Testa
The outer seed-coat layer.

Tegmen
An inner seed-coat layer.
The exact structure varies considerably among species.

16. Development of Cotyledons
Cotyledons are embryonic leaves.

Eudicots
Usually:
2 cotyledons  
Examples:
- Bean
- Pea
- Groundnut
- Soybean
- Chickpea

Monocots
Usually:
1 cotyledon  
In grasses, this is called the scutellum.

17. Food Reserve Accumulation
One of the most important parts of seed formation is accumulation of stored food.

Major reserves include:

Carbohydrates
Usually starch in many cereals.

Proteins
Important in pulses and many oilseeds.

Lipids
Important in oilseed crops.
The general process is:
Photosynthates→Transport→Developing seed→Storage reserves 
    

18. Source–Sink Relationship During Seed
Development
The developing seed is a strong sink.
Leaves act primarily as sources of photosynthetic assimilates.
Therefore:
Source leaf→Phloem→Developing seed     
The developing seed uses incoming assimilates for:
- Growth
- Respiration
- Protein synthesis
- Starch accumulation
- Oil accumulation

19. Seed Filling
Seed filling is the period during which the developing seed accumulates dry matter.
In cereals this is closely associated with grain filling.

During seed filling:
- Starch may accumulate
- Proteins accumulate
- Oils accumulate in oilseeds
- Dry matter increases
- Seed weight increases
Conceptually:
Seed filling→Dry matter accumulation→Increasing seed weight     

20. Seed Filling in Cereals
For crops such as:
- Wheat
- Rice
- Maize
- Barley
- Sorghum
starch accumulation in the endosperm is particularly important.
The process can be simplified as:
Photosynthesis→Sugars→Transport→Endosperm→Starch      This contributes strongly to final grain weight.

21. Seed Filling in Oilseeds
In crops such as:
- Soybean
- Groundnut
- Mustard
- Sunflower

- Sesame
the developing seed accumulates substantial amounts of oil.
A simplified relationship is:
Assimilates→Developing seed→Oil accumulation     
Protein accumulation also occurs.

22. Seed Filling in Pulses
Pulses such as:
- Chickpea
- Lentil
- Pea
- Pigeon pea
- Green gram
- Black gram
accumulate substantial protein and carbohydrate reserves in their seeds.

23. Seed Maturation
After major embryo and reserve development, the seed enters maturation.
During maturation:
- Embryo becomes fully developed
- Storage reserves accumulate
- Seed dry matter increases
- Water content generally declines
- Protective mechanisms develop
- Dormancy may develop
- Desiccation tolerance develops in many orthodox seeds

24. Physiological Maturity
Physiological maturity is generally the stage when the seed has reached approximately maximum dry
matter accumulation and has attained a high degree of developmental maturity.
Conceptually:
Physiological maturity≈Maximum seed dry matter    
The exact practical indicators vary among crops.

25. Harvest Maturity vs Physiological Maturity
These are not always identical.

Physiological maturity
Biological maturity of the seed.

Harvest maturity
The stage considered suitable for practical harvesting.
Harvest may occur somewhat after physiological maturity to allow appropriate drying or to reduce field
losses, depending on crop and production system.

26. Seed Desiccation
Many seeds undergo substantial water loss toward maturity.
This is called desiccation.
The process allows many orthodox seeds to enter a metabolically quiescent state suitable for storage.
Conceptually:
Maturing seed→Water loss→Metabolic slowdown→Dry mature seed 
    
Not all seeds tolerate drying equally.

27. Seed Dormancy
Some mature seeds enter a period in which they do not germinate immediately even under apparently
favorable conditions.
This is seed dormancy.
Dormancy can be advantageous because it prevents germination when conditions are unsuitable.
Possible mechanisms include:
- Hard seed coat
- Chemical inhibitors
- Embryo-related mechanisms
- Hormonal regulation
- Environmental requirements

28. Seed Maturity and Dormancy
The relationship can be represented as:
Seed development→Maturity→Dormancy in some species→Germination when conditions are appropr
iate      
Not every crop has the same degree or type of dormancy.

29. Seed Development and Hormones
Plant hormones regulate many aspects of seed development.
Important hormones include:

Auxins
Involved in:
- Embryo development
- Cell division
- Fruit/seed development

Cytokinins
Involved in:
- Cell division
- Nutrient mobilization
- Development

Gibberellins
Involved in:
- Growth
- Seed development
- Later germination processes

Abscisic acid (ABA)
Especially important in:
- Seed maturation
- Storage reserve accumulation
- Acquisition of desiccation tolerance
- Dormancy

Ethylene
Can influence:
- Development
- Maturation
- Senescence
Hormonal effects occur through complex interactions rather than a single-hormone mechanism.

30. Genetic Control of Seed Formation
Seed formation is controlled by coordinated genetic programs regulating:
- Cell division
- Cell differentiation
- Embryogenesis

- Endosperm development
- Seed coat development
- Storage reserve synthesis
- Dormancy
- Desiccation tolerance
Therefore:
Seed phenotype=Genetics+Environment+Developmental interactions  =
  +   +    

31. Maternal and Paternal Contributions
The developing seed has different genetic components.

Embryo
Usually contains genetic contributions from:
Maternal parent+Paternal parent +   

Endosperm
In the common angiosperm pattern, it contains:
2 maternal contributions+1 paternal contribution 
+1   
giving a typical:
3n 
genetic contribution.

Seed coat
The seed coat is derived from maternal ovule tissues and is therefore genetically maternal.
This distinction is very important in seed biology.

32. Maternal Tissue vs Embryo
A mature seed is therefore not genetically uniform.
For example:

Seed component
Main genetic origin
Embryo
Maternal + paternal
Endosperm
Typically 2 maternal : 1 paternal contributions
Seed coat
Maternal tissue
This is one reason seed development is biologically complex.

33. Seed Formation in Cereals
A cereal grain such as wheat, rice or maize develops after fertilization.
Simplified:
Flower→Pollination→Fertilization→Embryo + Endosperm→Grain filling→Mature grain        
The grain is botanically a caryopsis, a type of dry fruit in which the fruit wall is closely associated with
the seed coat.
Major components include:
- Bran/pericarp-seed coat region
- Endosperm
- Embryo/germ

34. Seed Formation in Legumes
In legumes such as chickpea, pea and soybean:
Flower→Fertilization→Pod development→Seed development     The developing seeds accumulate:
- Protein
- Carbohydrates
- Oil in some legumes
The pod develops from the ovary.

35. Seed Formation in Oilseeds
In oilseed crops:
Fertilization→Embryo development→Oil accumulation→Seed maturation 
   Examples:
- Mustard
- Soybean
- Sunflower
- Sesame
- Groundnut

36. Seed Formation in Fruits
In fruit crops:
Fertilization→Seed development+Fruit development   +    
For example, in mango:
- Ovule → seed
- Ovary → fruit
In tomato:
- Ovules → seeds
- Ovary → tomato fruit

37. Seed Formation vs Fruit Formation
These processes happen together but are not the same.

Seed formation
Primarily involves:
Ovule→Seed   

Fruit formation
Primarily involves:
Ovary→Fruit   
Therefore:
Seed≠Fruit   

38. Seed Formation vs Seed Germination
Seed formation
Occurs on the parent plant.
Fertilization→Seed development   Seed germination
Occurs after the mature seed is released/sown and begins developing into a new plant.
Mature seed→Water uptake→Germination    Thus:
Formation≠Germination   

39. Factors Affecting Seed Formation
Successful seed formation depends on:

Genetic factors
- Variety
- Fertility
- Compatibility
- Hybrid background

Pollination
- Pollen availability
- Pollinator activity
- Wind

- Pollen viability
- Stigma receptivity

Fertilization
- Pollen tube growth
- Compatibility
- Ovule health

Environment
- Temperature
- Water availability
- Humidity
- Light
- Wind

Plant nutrition
Especially adequate supply of nutrients required for reproductive development.

Pests and diseases
Damage to flowers or developing seeds can reduce seed formation.

40. Temperature and Seed Formation
Extreme temperature during flowering and early seed development can reduce reproductive success.
For example:
High temperature→Poor pollen function→Poor fertilization→Reduced seed set      
Later heat stress can also affect:
- Seed filling
- Grain weight
- Oil accumulation
- Protein accumulation

- Maturity

41. Water Stress During Seed Formation
Water stress during reproductive development can cause:
- Flower abortion
- Poor fertilization
- Reduced seed number
- Reduced seed size
- Premature senescence
- Reduced seed filling
The effect depends strongly on crop and developmental stage.

42. Nutrients and Seed Formation
Adequate nutrition supports:
- Flower production
- Fertilization
- Embryo development
- Seed filling
- Storage reserve accumulation
Nitrogen is particularly important for protein synthesis, while phosphorus and potassium have major
roles in energy metabolism, reproductive development, assimilate movement and overall plant
function.
However, excessive nutrient application can also be harmful and should be based on crop and soil
requirements.

43. Source–Sink Relationship
During seed formation:

Source
Primarily mature photosynthetic leaves.

Sink
Developing seeds, fruits and grains.
The simplified flow is:
Leaf photosynthesis→Sugars/assimilates→Phloem→Developing seed      
The seed converts these resources into:
- Starch
- Protein
- Oil
- Structural compounds

44. Seed Weight Formation
A simplified relationship is:
Final seed weight≈Seed growth rate×Duration of seed filling     
This is conceptual rather than a universal exact equation.
For cereals:
Grain yield≈Number of grains×Average grain weight     
Thus seed formation affects both major components of yield.

45. Seed Number and Seed Size
Yield can often be understood using:
Yield=Number of seeds×Average seed weight  =     
For a field crop, plant population and other components must also be considered.

There can be trade-offs between seed number and seed size depending on crop and environment.

46. Seed Development Stages
A useful generalized framework is:

Stage 1 — Fertilization
Formation of zygote and primary endosperm nucleus.

Stage 2 — Embryogenesis
Development of the embryo.

Stage 3 — Endosperm development
Development of nutritional tissue.

Stage 4 — Seed coat development
Protective layers mature.

Stage 5 — Reserve accumulation
Starch, protein and/or oil accumulate.

Stage 6 — Maturation
Seed reaches developmental maturity.

Stage 7 — Desiccation
Water content declines in many orthodox seeds.

Stage 8 — Dormancy/quiescence
The mature seed becomes capable of surviving until suitable germination conditions occur.

48. Seed Formation and Seed Quality
The conditions under which seeds develop can influence their eventual quality.
Good seed development can contribute to:
- High viability
- High vigour
- Good germination
- Better seedling establishment
- Adequate storage reserves
Stress during seed development may reduce:
- Seed size
- Germination
- Vigour

- Longevity
- Seed quality
Therefore:
Seed quality begins before harvest   
It is influenced by crop management throughout seed development.

49. Seed Development and Seed Longevity
Seed longevity depends on the physiological state of the seed at maturity as well as subsequent storage.
Factors include:
- Maturity at harvest
- Moisture content
- Damage
- Genetic characteristics
- Storage conditions
Properly matured and handled seeds generally have better storage potential than immature or damaged
seeds.

50. Important Terms

Seed formation
Ovule
Zygote
Embryo

Seed coat

Embryonic leaf

Desiccation

51. Most Important Relationships to Remember
Relationship 1
Ovule→Seed   

Relationship 2
Ovary→Fruit   

Relationship 3
Zygote→Embryo   

Relationship 4
Primary endosperm nucleus→Endosperm 
  

Relationship 5
Integuments→Seed coat   

Relationship 6
Embryo+Endosperm+Seed coat→Mature seed + +    

52. Seed Formation vs Fertilization
These two topics are closely related but different.
Fertilization
Seed formation
A specific reproductive event
Developmental process
Male + female gamete fusion
Development of the fertilized ovule
Produces zygote and initiates endosperm formation Produces mature seed
Relatively brief event
Extended developmental period

Fertilization
Occurs within embryo sac
Thus:

Seed formation
Continues within developing ovule

Fertilization is the trigger; seed formation is the subsequent developmental process.
   

53. Seed Formation vs Seed Maturation
Seed formation
The broad process of developing the seed structure.

Seed maturation
The later phase in which the seed:
- Accumulates reserves
- Reaches developmental maturity
- Loses water
- Acquires desiccation tolerance in many species
- May develop dormancy
So:
Seed formation⊃Seed maturation    
in the broad developmental sense.

54. Complete Crop Reproductive Sequence
You can connect all the previous topics as:
Flower development↓Flowering↓Pollination↓Pollen germination↓Pollen tube growth↓Double fertilizati
- n↓Zygote + Endosperm formation↓Embryo development↓Seed coat development↓Reserve accumulati
- n↓Seed maturation↓Mature seed↓Germination↓Seedling↓Mature plant 
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\ & \\
& \\ & \\ & \\ &     
This forms a complete connection between flowering → pollination → fertilization → seed
formation → germination.

55. Final Conceptual Takeaway
Seed formation is the developmental process by which a fertilized ovule becomes a mature seed.
The three most important transformations are:
Zygote→Embryo   
Primary endosperm nucleus→Endosperm 
   Ovule integuments→Seed coat 
  
Together:
Embryo+Storage tissue+Seed coat→Mature Seed  +   +
    
And the complete sequence is:
Pollination→Fertilization→Embryo + Endosperm→Seed Development→Seed Filling→Maturation→
Mature Seed→Germination          
In one sentence:
Seed formation begins after fertilization and involves coordinated development of the
embryo, endosperm, seed coat and stored reserves until the ovule becomes a mature,
viable seed capable of producing the next generation.

Photosynthesis in Plants
## 1. Introduction
Photosynthesis is the biological process by which green plants capture light energy and use it to
convert carbon dioxide (CO₂) and water (H₂O) into energy-rich organic compounds, primarily
carbohydrates, while releasing oxygen (O₂) as a by-product.
In simplified form:

6CO2+6H2O+light energy→C6H12O6+6O2   C 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  
Photosynthesis is one of the most important processes in agriculture because it is the fundamental
source of plant biomass, food, fiber, oils, fruits, seeds and many other agricultural products.
genui "learning viz": "type id":"PHOTOSYNTHESIS","initial values":
 "lightIntensity":"medium","carbonDioxide":"medium","water":"medium"   

## 2. Definition
Photosynthesis is the process by which photoautotrophic organisms use light energy to
synthesize organic compounds from carbon dioxide and water.
In higher green plants, photosynthesis primarily occurs in the leaves, especially in cells containing
chloroplasts.
The basic concept is:
Light energy→Chemical energy→Organic matter     

3. Why Is Photosynthesis Important?
Photosynthesis provides the foundation for:
- Plant growth
- Biomass production
- Crop yield
- Food production
- Oxygen production
- Carbon fixation
- Formation of carbohydrates
- Production of precursors for proteins, fats and other compounds
- Agricultural productivity
- Terrestrial food chains
A simplified agricultural relationship is:

Photosynthesis→Biomass→Economic yield     
For example:
- Rice → grain
- Wheat → grain
- Maize → grain
- Potato → tuber
- Sugarcane → stem sugar
- Cotton → fiber
- Soybean → seed/oil/protein
- Fruit crops → fruits

4. Photosynthesis as the Primary CarbonFixation Process
Plants take inorganic carbon from atmospheric CO₂ and incorporate it into organic molecules.
Therefore:
CO2→Organic carbon   
This process is called carbon fixation.
The carbon eventually becomes part of:
- Sugars
- Starch
- Cellulose
- Proteins
- Lipids
- Organic acids
- Plant structural material

5. Where Does Photosynthesis Occur?
Photosynthesis primarily occurs in chloroplasts.
Chloroplasts are specialized organelles found in photosynthetic cells.
A simplified chloroplast structure is:
## Chloroplast
│
┌──────────┴──────────┐
│
│
Stroma
Thylakoids
│
│
Calvin cycle
Light reactions
│
Grana

6. Structure of the Chloroplast
A chloroplast has several important components.

Outer membrane
Provides an outer boundary.

Inner membrane
Controls movement of substances into and out of the chloroplast.

Stroma
The fluid-filled region surrounding the thylakoid system.
The major carbon-fixation reactions occur here.

Thylakoid membrane
Contains:
- Chlorophyll
- Photosystems
- Electron transport components
- ATP-producing machinery
The light-dependent reactions occur here.

Grana
Stacks of thylakoids.
Singular:
Granum 
Plural:
Grana 

Stroma lamellae
Membranous connections between grana.

## 7. Chlorophyll
Chlorophyll is the major photosynthetic pigment in green plants.
Important forms include:
- Chlorophyll a
- Chlorophyll b

Chlorophyll a
The principal reaction-center pigment in oxygenic photosynthesis.

Chlorophyll b
An accessory pigment that helps absorb light and transfer energy toward the photosynthetic reaction
centers.

8. Other Photosynthetic Pigments
Plants also contain accessory pigments such as:

Carotenoids
Examples:
- Carotenes
- Xanthophylls

Their functions include:
- Absorbing additional wavelengths of light
- Transferring energy to photosynthetic systems
- Helping protect photosynthetic machinery from excess light

9. Why Are Plants Green?
Chlorophyll absorbs much of the:
- Blue region of visible light
- Red region of visible light
It reflects/transmits relatively more green light.
Therefore leaves appear green.
This is why:
Chlorophyll absorption pattern→Green appearance 
  

10. Main Raw Materials
Photosynthesis requires several inputs.

Carbon dioxide
Main carbon source.
CO2CO 2
It enters mainly through stomata.

Water
Absorbed primarily by roots and transported to leaves.
H2OH 2O

Light
Provides the energy required to drive photosynthetic reactions.

Photosynthetic machinery
Includes:
- Chlorophyll
- Photosystems
- Electron carriers
- Enzymes
- Chloroplast structures

11. Overall Photosynthesis Equation
The simplified overall equation is:
6CO2+6H2O→lightC6H12O6+6O2   C 6H  12 
O 6+6O 2  
However, this equation is a simplified representation of a much more complex set of reactions.
Photosynthesis does not simply occur as one single chemical reaction.
It consists of interconnected reaction pathways.

12. Two Major Stages of Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis is broadly divided into:

1. Light-dependent reactions
Occur in the thylakoid membranes.
They convert light energy into chemical energy, producing:
- ATP
## • Nadph
and release oxygen through water splitting.

2. Light-independent reactions
Commonly called the Calvin cycle.
They occur primarily in the stroma.

They use:
- CO₂
- ATP
## • Nadph
to synthesize carbohydrate precursors.
Thus:
Light reactions→ATP+NADPH   ATP+NADPH  
followed by:
ATP+NADPH+CO2→Carbohydrate   

13. Important Clarification: "Dark Reaction"
The Calvin cycle is sometimes called the dark reaction.
This terminology can be misleading.
It does not mean that the plant must be in darkness.
It means that the Calvin cycle does not directly use photons in the individual reactions.
However, it depends on ATP and NADPH generated by the light reactions, so photosynthetic carbon
fixation normally operates in coordination with light-dependent processes.
Therefore, a better term is:
Light-independent reactions  

14. Light-Dependent Reactions
The light reactions begin when pigments absorb photons.
The simplified pathway is:
Light→Photosystem II→Electron transport→Photosystem I→ATP+NADPH 
    ATP+NADPH  
Oxygen is produced through water splitting associated with Photosystem II.

15. Photosystem II
Photosystem II is the first major photosystem in the conventional electron-flow sequence.
It absorbs light energy and drives electron transfer.
Water is split to replace electrons lost from the reaction center.
Simplified:
2H2O→4H++4e−+O2 
Therefore:
Water is the source of oxygen released during oxygenic photosynthesis   
This is an important concept.
The oxygen released during photosynthesis comes from water, not directly from CO₂.

16. Electron Transport Chain
Excited electrons move through a series of electron carriers.
Their movement contributes to the establishment of a proton gradient across the thylakoid membrane.
The proton gradient drives ATP synthesis.
Conceptually:
Electron transport→Proton gradient→ATP synthesis     

17. ATP Formation
ATP is produced through photophosphorylation.
The enzyme ATP synthase uses the proton gradient to convert:
ADP+Pi→ATPADP+P i ATP
Thus:
Light energy→Electron transport→Proton gradient→ATP     ATP  

18. Photosystem I
Photosystem I absorbs light and boosts electron energy again.
The high-energy electrons ultimately contribute to the reduction of NADP⁺:
NADP++H++2e−→NADPH 
NADPH provides reducing power for carbon fixation.

19. ATP and NADPH
The light reactions produce two major chemical energy products:
ATP 
and
NADPH 
Their roles are broadly:
Molecule
Major role
ATP
Provides usable chemical energy
NADPH Provides reducing power
The Calvin cycle uses both.

20. Linear Electron Flow
The conventional pathway is:
H2O→PSII→Electron carriers→PSI→NADPH 
At the same time, the process produces ATP.
Products include:
- ATP
## • Nadph
- O₂

21. Cyclic Electron Flow
Electrons can also undergo cyclic electron flow around Photosystem I.
This pathway primarily helps generate additional ATP without producing NADPH directly and without
splitting water in that cycle.
Therefore:
Cyclic flow→additional ATP    
It helps balance the ATP/NADPH requirements of carbon fixation.

22. Calvin Cycle
The Calvin cycle is the major carbon-fixation pathway of C₃ plants.
It occurs in the chloroplast stroma.
It can be divided into three major phases:
1. Carbon fixation
## 2. Reduction
3. Regeneration of RuBP

23. Phase 1 — Carbon Fixation
CO₂ combines with a five-carbon molecule called RuBP.
The enzyme responsible is:
RuBisCO  
Full name:
Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate carboxylase/oxygenase
The initial unstable product rapidly forms molecules of 3-phosphoglycerate (3-PGA).
For each CO₂ fixed:
CO2+RuBP→3-PGA   

24. Phase 2 — Reduction
3-PGA is converted through several reactions using:
- ATP
## • Nadph
into glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate (G3P).
Conceptually:
3-PGA+ATP+NADPH→G3P  + ATP + NADPH  G3P  
G3P can be used to form carbohydrates and other organic compounds.

25. Phase 3 — Regeneration
Most G3P molecules remain within the Calvin cycle and are used to regenerate RuBP.
This allows the cycle to continue.
A smaller fraction of G3P exits the cycle for carbohydrate synthesis.
Therefore:
G3P→ Carbohydrate synthesisRuBP regeneration 
 \\

27. What Is the Final Product?
The immediate carbohydrate product leaving the Calvin cycle is primarily G3P, not directly a sixcarbon glucose molecule.
G3P can subsequently contribute to the synthesis of:
- Glucose
- Sucrose
- Starch
- Cellulose
- Other organic compounds
Therefore:
Calvin cycle→G3P→Carbohydrates and other metabolites   G3P
  

28. Photosynthesis and Glucose
Glucose is often used in simplified equations because it is easy to understand.
In actual plant metabolism, photosynthetic carbon is distributed into many compounds.
For example:
G3P→ SucroseStarchCelluloseOrganic acidsAmino-acid precursorsLipidsG3P  \\  \\  \\  \\  \\    

29. Sucrose and Starch
Sucrose
A major transport sugar.
It is transported through the phloem from source tissues to sinks.
Source leaf→Sucrose→Phloem→Sink      

Starch
A major storage carbohydrate.
It can accumulate in:
- Leaves
- Seeds
- Tubers
- Roots
- Stems

30. Photosynthesis and Plant Respiration
Photosynthesis and respiration are closely connected but are not the same process.

Photosynthesis
CO2+H2O+light→organic matter+O2CO 2+H 2O+   +O 2

Aerobic respiration
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+energyC 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
 
Photosynthesis stores energy in organic molecules.
Respiration releases usable energy from organic molecules.
Plants perform respiration continuously, including during the night.

31. Photosynthesis and Respiration Together
A simplified relationship is:
Photosynthesis→Carbon gain    
Respiration→Carbon expenditure    
Net biomass accumulation depends on the balance between carbon fixation and carbon losses through
respiration and other processes.

32. Photosynthesis and Transpiration
Photosynthesis requires CO₂.
CO₂ enters primarily through stomata.
But open stomata also allow water vapor to escape.
Therefore there is a fundamental physiological trade-off:
CO2 uptake↔Water loss  2    
This connects:
- Photosynthesis
- Stomatal conductance
- Transpiration
- Water-use efficiency

## 33. Stomata
Stomata are microscopic pores, usually found mainly on leaf surfaces.
Each stoma is controlled by guard cells.
They regulate:
- CO₂ entry
- O₂ exchange
- Water vapor loss
Simplified:
Atmosphere
│
CO₂
↓
## [ Stoma ]
↓
Leaf internal air spaces
↓
Mesophyll cells
↓
Chloroplast
↓
Carbon fixation

34. Guard Cells
Guard cells control stomatal opening and closing.

When stomata open
Generally:
CO2 entry↑CO 2  but:
H2O loss↑H 2O  When stomata close
Generally:
H2O loss↓H 2O  but:
CO2 entry↓CO 2  Therefore plants constantly balance carbon gain against water conservation.

35. Photosynthesis in Mesophyll Cells
Most photosynthesis in leaves occurs in mesophyll cells.
These cells contain chloroplasts.
The general pathway is:
CO2→Stomata→Intercellular air spaces→Mesophyll→Chloroplast      

36. Light as an Input
Light provides energy for photosynthesis.
Important light characteristics include:
- Intensity
- Quality/wavelength
- Duration

- Direction
Photosynthetically useful radiation is generally referred to as photosynthetically active radiation
(PAR), approximately 400–700 nm.

37. Light Intensity
As light intensity increases, photosynthetic rate generally increases initially.
However, eventually the rate reaches a plateau.
Conceptually:
Light intensity↑→Photosynthesis↑     
until other limitations become dominant.
At very excessive light, photosynthetic machinery can suffer photoinhibition or photodamage.

38. Light Compensation Point
The light compensation point is the light intensity at which:
Photosynthetic CO₂ fixation≈Respiratory CO₂ release 
  
Therefore net carbon exchange is approximately zero.
Below this point, respiration may exceed photosynthetic carbon gain.

39. Light Saturation Point
The light saturation point is the approximate light level beyond which increasing light produces little
additional photosynthesis under the given conditions.
Therefore:
More light⇏unlimited photosynthesis    

40. Carbon Dioxide
CO₂ is the primary carbon source for C₃ and C₄ photosynthesis.
Generally:
CO2↑→Photosynthesis↑CO 2   within a suitable range and provided other factors are not limiting.
Eventually another factor becomes limiting.

## 41. Water
Water is essential for photosynthesis.
It:
- Provides electrons for the light reactions
- Provides hydrogen/reducing equivalents through the photosynthetic system
- Maintains cell hydration
- Supports stomatal function
- Supports transport and metabolism
Water stress can reduce photosynthesis through several mechanisms.

42. Photosynthesis and Water Stress
During drought:
Water stress→Stomatal closure→CO2 entry↓→Photosynthesis↓    CO 2    Severe stress can additionally damage photosynthetic machinery and alter metabolism.

## 43. Temperature
Photosynthesis is strongly affected by temperature.
A generalized response is:

Low temperature→enzyme activity↓    Moderate suitable temperature→higher photosynthetic activity   Excessive temperature→photosynthetic stress   The optimum varies with:
- Species
- Cultivar
- C₃/C₄ pathway
- Acclimation
- Growth conditions

44. Mineral Nutrients and Photosynthesis
Photosynthesis depends indirectly and directly on mineral nutrition.

Nitrogen
Required for:
- Chlorophyll-associated proteins
- Rubisco
- Enzymes
- Leaf development

Magnesium
Central atom in the chlorophyll molecule.

Iron
Important for electron transport proteins.

Manganese
Important in the oxygen-evolving complex of Photosystem II.

Phosphorus
Important for ATP and metabolic processes.

Potassium
Important for stomatal regulation and enzyme activation.
Therefore nutrient deficiencies can reduce photosynthetic capacity.

45. C₃ Photosynthesis
C₃ photosynthesis is the most widespread carbon-fixation pathway.
The first stable product of CO₂ fixation is a three-carbon compound, 3-PGA.
Examples include:
- Rice
- Wheat
- Soybean
- Potato
- Chickpea
- Tomato
- Many temperate crops
The enzyme RuBisCO directly fixes CO₂ onto RuBP.

46. C₄ Photosynthesis
C₄ plants first fix CO₂ into a four-carbon compound.
Examples:
- Maize
- Sugarcane
- Sorghum
- Pearl millet
C₄ photosynthesis uses a spatial separation of initial carbon fixation and the Calvin cycle.
A simplified pathway:

CO2→4-carbon compound→Bundle sheath→CO2 concentration→Calvin cycleCO 2    CO 2 
 47. Why Are C₄ Plants Efficient in Hot
Conditions?
C₄ plants concentrate CO₂ around RuBisCO.
This reduces the relative impact of RuBisCO's oxygenase activity and photorespiration.
C₄ plants are therefore often well adapted to:
- High temperature
- High light
- Conditions where photorespiration would otherwise be substantial
But C₄ does not mean that every C₄ crop will outperform every C₃ crop under every environment.

49. CAM Photosynthesis
CAM = Crassulacean Acid Metabolism
It is an adaptation found in plants exposed to water limitation.
Examples include:
- Pineapple

- Many succulents
CAM plants generally:

At night
Open stomata and take up CO₂.

During the day
Stomata are more closed, conserving water, while stored CO₂ is used for carbon fixation.
Therefore:
CAM=Temporal separation of CO₂ uptake and carbon fixation  =

## 51. Photorespiration
RuBisCO has two activities:
## 1. Carboxylase
## 2. Oxygenase
When RuBisCO reacts with O₂ instead of CO₂, photorespiration is initiated.
This can reduce net carbon fixation.
It becomes more significant under conditions such as:
- High temperature
- Low internal CO₂
- High O₂ relative to CO₂
C₄ plants reduce this problem through their CO₂-concentrating mechanism.

52. Photosynthesis and Crop Yield
Photosynthesis is fundamental to biomass production.
A conceptual chain is:
Light+CO2+H2O→Photosynthesis→Assimilates→Biomass→Yield  + CO 2 +
H 2O      
But photosynthesis alone does not determine yield.
Yield also depends on:
- Respiration
- Partitioning
- Reproductive success
- Sink strength
- Crop duration
- Genetics
- Water
- Nutrients
- Temperature
- Pests and diseases

53. Source and Sink
This is particularly important in crop science.

Source
An organ producing/exporting assimilates.
Examples:
- Mature leaves

Sink
An organ importing/using/storing assimilates.
Examples:

- Developing seeds
- Fruits
- Tubers
- Roots
- Growing shoots
The relationship is:
Source→Phloem→Sink     

54. Photosynthesis and Grain Yield
In cereals:
Leaf photosynthesis→Assimilate production→Grain filling→Grain yield      
Important leaves can include:
- Flag leaf
- Upper canopy leaves
However, developing grains can also receive assimilates remobilized from stored reserves elsewhere in
the plant.

55. Photosynthesis and Fruit Yield
In fruit crops:
Leaf photosynthesis→Carbohydrates→Fruit growth→Fruit yield 
   Fruit development depends on:
- Photosynthetic supply
- Fruit number
- Sink strength
- Water
- Nutrients

- Hormones
- Temperature

56. Photosynthesis and Root Growth
Roots do not generally perform as much photosynthesis as green leaves.
They depend substantially on carbohydrates transported from shoots.
Leaf photosynthesis→Assimilates→Roots     
This supports:
- Root respiration
- Root growth
- Nutrient uptake
- Root–microbe interactions

57. Photosynthesis and Biomass
A simplified conceptual model is:
Gross photosynthesis−Respiration−Other carbon losses=Net biomass accumulation  -   -   =    
This is not a complete crop growth equation, but it captures the basic relationship.

58. Leaf Area and Photosynthesis
The amount of leaf area strongly influences canopy photosynthesis.
A useful measure is:
LAI=Total one-sided leaf areaGround area  
     
where LAI = Leaf Area Index.
For example:
If a 1 m² ground area supports 3 m² of leaf area:

LAI=31=3LAI=  1 =3

59. LAI and Canopy Photosynthesis
As LAI increases:

Initially
More leaf area:
→more light interception→higher canopy photosynthesis  Excessively high LAI
Can cause:
- Self-shading
- Poor light penetration
- Higher humidity
- Increased disease risk
- Increased maintenance respiration
Therefore:
Maximum leaf area≠always maximum productivity   
An efficient canopy balances light capture and respiration.

60. Photosynthetic Rate
Photosynthetic rate is commonly expressed as the amount of CO₂ assimilated per unit leaf area per unit
time.
For example:
μmol CO2 m−2 s−1 mol\ CO 2\ m  -2 \ s  -1 
It can be measured using gas-exchange systems.

61. Factors Affecting Photosynthetic Rate
The major factors are:
Light+CO2+Temperature+Water+Nutrients+Leaf condition  + CO 2 +
  +   +   +    
Other factors include:
- Humidity
- Stomatal conductance
- Leaf age
- Disease
- Canopy architecture
- Atmospheric conditions

62. Law of Limiting Factors
Photosynthesis is controlled by the factor that is most limiting under the current conditions.
For example:
If light is high but water is extremely low:
Water limitation→Photosynthesis limited    
If water and light are adequate but CO₂ availability is low:
CO2 limitation→Photosynthesis limited    
Therefore:
Increasing one factor cannot indefinitely compensate for another limiting factor   

63. Photosynthesis and Drought
Drought affects photosynthesis through several pathways:

Drought→ABA signaling→Stomatal closure→CO2 uptake↓→Photosynthesis↓ 
   CO 2 
   Severe drought can additionally cause:
- Leaf rolling
- Reduced leaf area
- Premature senescence
- Membrane damage
- Chloroplast damage

64. Photosynthesis and Waterlogging
Waterlogging can reduce root oxygen availability.
This affects:
- Root respiration
- Nutrient uptake
- Water relations
- Hormonal signaling
Eventually leaf photosynthesis can decline.
Thus:
Waterlogging→Root stress→Reduced plant function→Reduced photosynthesis      

65. Photosynthesis and Nutrient Deficiency
Nitrogen deficiency
Can cause:
- Reduced chlorophyll
- Pale leaves
- Reduced photosynthetic capacity

Magnesium deficiency
Can interfere with chlorophyll formation and function.

Iron deficiency
Can impair chloroplast development and electron transport.

Potassium deficiency
Can impair stomatal regulation and other physiological processes.
Thus:
Nutrient deficiency→Leaf dysfunction→Reduced photosynthesis 
   

66. Photosynthesis and Leaf Age
Photosynthetic capacity changes with leaf age.

Young leaves
May have incomplete photosynthetic development.

Mature leaves
Often reach high photosynthetic capacity.

Old/senescing leaves
Photosynthetic capacity generally declines.
Therefore:
Leaf age→Photosynthetic capacity changes   

67. Photosynthesis and Senescence
During senescence:
- Chlorophyll degrades
- Photosynthetic proteins decline
- Nutrients are remobilized
- Photosynthetic capacity decreases

The plant may transfer nutrients from older leaves to:
- Seeds
- Fruits
- Growing organs
Thus senescence is not simply "death"; it can be a regulated nutrient-remobilization process.

68. Photosynthesis and Carbon Allocation
The carbon fixed during photosynthesis can be allocated toward:
Growth+Respiration+Storage+Defense+Reproduction  +   +
  +   +    
Therefore, increased photosynthesis does not automatically mean the same proportional increase in
harvested yield.
The plant must also partition assimilates effectively.

69. Photosynthesis and Agricultural
Management
Farmers influence photosynthesis indirectly through:

Proper irrigation
Maintains plant water status.

Balanced fertilization
Supports leaf development and photosynthetic machinery.

Proper plant density
Controls canopy structure and light interception.

Weed management
Reduces competition for:
- Light
- Water

- Nutrients

Pest and disease management
Maintains healthy photosynthetic leaf area.

Appropriate variety
Genotypes differ in:
- Photosynthetic capacity
- Canopy architecture
- Heat tolerance
- Drought tolerance
- Stay-green characteristics

70. Photosynthetic Efficiency
Photosynthetic efficiency refers broadly to how effectively plants convert available light energy into
chemical energy/biomass.
It is influenced by:
- Light interception
- Photosynthetic machinery
- CO₂ concentration
- Temperature
- Water availability
- Nutrients
- Respiration
- Canopy architecture
- Carbon partitioning

71. Water-Use Efficiency
Because stomata regulate both CO₂ uptake and water loss, photosynthesis is closely related to wateruse efficiency.

A simplified physiological concept is:
WUE≈Carbon assimilationWater loss  
     
The exact definition varies depending on whether it is calculated at leaf, plant or crop scale.

72. Photosynthesis and Carbon Sequestration
Plants remove atmospheric CO₂ through photosynthetic carbon fixation.
Some of this carbon is incorporated into:
- Leaves
- Stems
- Roots
- Seeds
- Fruits
- Soil organic matter after plant residues enter the soil
Therefore:
Photosynthesis→Biological carbon fixation    
However, carbon can later return to the atmosphere through respiration, decomposition and
combustion.

73. Photosynthesis and Agriculture
Agricultural productivity ultimately depends heavily on capturing solar energy.
A useful conceptual hierarchy is:
Sunlight→Photosynthesis→Carbon fixation→Biomass→Harvestable product 
     
For example:

Rice
Photosynthesis→carbohydrates→grain   Potato
Photosynthesis→carbohydrates→tuber   Sugarcane
Photosynthesis→sucrose→stem   Cotton
Photosynthesis→biomass→fiber + seed   74. Common Misconceptions
"Plants only photosynthesize during the day."
Photosynthesis requires light, so the light reactions require light. The Calvin cycle depends on products
generated by light reactions and normally operates during illuminated periods.
Plants also respire continuously, day and night.

"Photosynthesis occurs only in leaves."
Leaves are the major site in many plants, but other green organs can photosynthesize.
For example:
- Green stems
- Green fruits
- Young green tissues

"Oxygen comes from CO₂."
The oxygen released during oxygenic photosynthesis originates from water splitting.

"Glucose is directly produced in one step."
No.
The Calvin cycle first produces G3P and related intermediates, which can subsequently contribute to
glucose, sucrose, starch and many other compounds.

"More fertilizer always means more photosynthesis."
Incorrect.
Nutrients are necessary, but excessive fertilizer can damage plants, disturb nutrient balance and create
environmental problems.

"More leaves always mean more yield."
Not necessarily.
Excessive leaf area can cause:
- Self-shading
- High respiration
- Poor canopy ventilation
- Disease-favorable conditions
Efficient canopy architecture matters.

79. Final Takeaway
Photosynthesis is the foundation of crop productivity.
At the simplest level:
Light+CO2+H2O→Organic carbon+O2 +CO 2+H 2O  +O 2  
But biologically, it is a highly coordinated process involving:
Light capture→Electron transport→ATP+NADPH→CO2 fixation→G3P→Carbohydrates→Biomass - xed      ATP+NADPH  CO 2   G3P    
The agricultural significance is:
Photosynthesis→Biomass→Seeds/Fruits/Tubers/Stems/Fiber→Crop Yield      
The most important factors controlling photosynthesis are:
Light + CO₂ + Water + Temperature + Nutrients + Leaf/Canopy condition   
And the key crop-science principle is:
Photosynthesis captures solar energy and converts atmospheric carbon into organic
matter; how much of that captured carbon ultimately becomes harvested yield
depends on respiration, growth, source–sink relationships, reproductive success,
carbon partitioning, genetics and the growing environment.

Respiration in Plants
## 1. Introduction
Respiration is the metabolic process through which plant cells break down organic compounds, mainly
carbohydrates, and release energy that can be captured in usable forms such as ATP.
A simplified equation for aerobic respiration is:
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2O+Energy O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O+
   
The released energy is not simply "lost as heat." A significant portion is captured in ATP, which powers
cellular activities.
In agriculture, respiration is extremely important because plants must continuously respire to:

- Grow
- Absorb nutrients
- Maintain cells
- Transport substances
- Develop roots, stems, leaves, flowers and seeds
- Produce fruits and storage organs
- Germinate seeds
- Respond to stress
- Maintain living tissues after harvest

## 2. Definition
Respiration is the series of biochemical reactions by which cells oxidize organic
substrates and capture released energy in forms such as ATP for cellular work.
The most important substrates include:
- Carbohydrates
- Organic acids
- Lipids
- Proteins under certain conditions
The main respiratory substrate in the simplified equation is glucose.

3. Respiration Is Not the Same as Breathing
This distinction is very important.

Breathing / gas exchange
Refers mainly to movement of gases:
O2↔CO2O 2 CO 2

Cellular respiration
Refers to biochemical reactions that extract energy from organic molecules.

Therefore:
Breathing≠Cellular respiration    
A plant does not have lungs. Gases move through structures such as:
- Stomata
- Lenticels
- Root surfaces
- Intercellular spaces

4. Why Do Plants Respire?
Plants need energy continuously.
ATP generated through respiration supports:
- Active transport
- Ion uptake
- Mineral absorption
- Biosynthesis
- Cell division
- Cell expansion
- Protein synthesis
- DNA/RNA synthesis
- Membrane maintenance
- Phloem loading
- Root growth
- Seed germination
- Reproductive development
- Repair
- Stress responses
Thus:
Respiration→ATP→Cellular work     

5. When Does Respiration Occur?
Plants respire:
Day and night  
This is different from photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis requires light, but respiration occurs continuously in living cells.
During the day:
Photosynthesis+Respiration + 
During the night:
Respiration continues

7. Where Does Respiration Occur?
Different stages occur in different cellular locations.

Glycolysis

Cytosol

## 8. Mitochondria
The mitochondrion is the major organelle associated with aerobic respiration.
Important structures include:
- Outer membrane
- Intermembrane space
- Inner membrane
- Cristae

- Matrix

Cristae
Folds of the inner mitochondrial membrane.
They increase membrane surface area for respiratory electron transport and ATP synthesis.

Matrix
Contains enzymes involved in:
- Pyruvate oxidation
- Krebs cycle

9. Three Major Stages of Aerobic Respiration
For simplified plant respiration, the major stages are:
Glycolysis→Pyruvate oxidation + Krebs cycle→Electron transport + oxidative phosphorylation        
These stages are connected but chemically distinct.

10. Stage 1 — Glycolysis
Glycolysis literally means:
Glyco=sugar =  lysis=splitting = 
It occurs in the cytosol.
One six-carbon glucose molecule is converted into two three-carbon pyruvate molecules.
Simplified:
Glucose→2Pyruvate 

11. Energy Investment in Glycolysis
The pathway initially uses ATP.
Therefore, ATP is invested to activate glucose and its intermediates.

Later reactions produce ATP.
This gives a net gain of ATP.
For one glucose molecule, classical accounting gives:
Net ATP from glycolysis=2 =2 
Glycolysis also produces:
2NADH 
and:
2Pyruvate 

12. Overall Simplified Glycolysis
A simplified representation is:
Glucose+2ADP+2Pi+2NAD+→2Pyruvate+2ATP+2NADH+water   
The exact biochemical pathway contains many intermediate reactions.

13. Stage 2 — Pyruvate Oxidation
Under aerobic conditions, pyruvate enters mitochondria.
Each pyruvate is converted into:
Acetyl−CoA 
During this process:
- CO₂ is released
- NADH is produced
For two pyruvate molecules:
2Pyruvate→2Acetyl−CoA+2CO2+2NADH 

14. Stage 3 — Krebs Cycle
The Krebs cycle is also called:

- Citric acid cycle
- TCA cycle
TCA stands for:
Tricarboxylic acid cycle
It occurs mainly in the mitochondrial matrix.
Acetyl-CoA enters the cycle.
The cycle generates:
## • Nadh
## • Fadh₂
- ATP or an equivalent phosphorylated energy molecule
- CO₂

15. Simplified Krebs Cycle
Acetyl-CoA
│
↓
Citrate
│
↓
Several reactions
│
┌────────────┼────────────┐
↓
↓
↓
## Nadh
## Fadh₂
ATP
│
│
└──────┬─────┘
↓
Electron transport chain
CO₂ is released during
- xidative reactions

The actual cycle contains multiple intermediates and enzyme-catalyzed reactions.

16. What Is the Purpose of the Krebs Cycle?
The Krebs cycle does not simply exist to "make ATP."
A major function is to generate reduced electron carriers:

NADH 
and
FADH2 
These carry high-energy electrons to the electron transport chain.
Therefore:
Krebs cycle→NADH+FADH2→ETC   NADH+FADH 2
  

17. Electron Transport Chain
The electron transport chain (ETC) is located in the inner mitochondrial membrane.
NADH and FADH₂ donate electrons.
Electrons pass through several protein complexes and electron carriers.
Their energy is used to pump protons across the inner mitochondrial membrane.
This establishes a proton gradient.

18. Proton Gradient
The electron transport chain creates a difference in proton concentration across the inner mitochondrial
membrane.
This is:
Proton motive force  
The stored electrochemical energy drives ATP synthesis.

19. ATP Synthase
Protons flow back across the membrane through ATP synthase.
This drives:
ADP+Pi→ATP 
This process is called:
Oxidative phosphorylation  

20. Role of Oxygen
In aerobic respiration, oxygen is the terminal electron acceptor.
At the end of the electron transport chain:
O2+e−+H+→H2O 
Therefore oxygen allows electron transport to continue efficiently.
This is why severe oxygen deficiency can strongly disrupt aerobic respiration.

21. Complete Aerobic Respiration
A simplified flow is:
Glucose→Glycolysis→Pyruvate→Acetyl−CoA→Krebs cycle→NADH/
FADH2→ETC→ATP 

22. Energy Yield
The commonly taught simplified value for complete oxidation of one glucose molecule in eukaryotic
cells is approximately:
30−32 ATP 
The exact ATP yield is not a universal fixed number because it depends on:
- Cellular conditions
- Electron-shuttle systems
- Proton leak
- Transport costs
- Metabolic state
Older textbooks may give values such as 36 or 38 ATP.
For modern understanding, 30–32 ATP per glucose is a commonly used estimate for many eukaryotic
cells.

23. Where Does the Energy Come From?
The energy ultimately comes from chemical bonds and redox reactions involving organic substrates.
A simplified sequence is:
Organic substrate→Electron carriers→Electron transport→Proton gradient→ATP 

24. ATP — The Energy Currency
ATP stands for:
Adenosine triphosphate
It stores transferable chemical energy.
ATP can be hydrolyzed:
ATP→ADP+Pi+usable energy   
The released energy supports cellular processes.

25. Aerobic Respiration
Aerobic respiration uses oxygen as the terminal electron acceptor.
Simplified:
Organic substrate+O2→CO2+H2O+ATP   
It generally provides much more ATP per glucose molecule than fermentation.

26. Anaerobic Respiration and Fermentation
When oxygen availability becomes severely limited, normal mitochondrial electron transport cannot
continue at its usual rate.
Plants may shift toward fermentative metabolism.
Common pathways include:

Alcoholic fermentation
Pyruvate→Acetaldehyde→EthanolPyruvate  Acetaldehyde  Ethanol
with CO₂ release.

Lactic acid fermentation
Can occur in some plant tissues under particular oxygen-limited conditions, although plant responses
vary.

## 27. Fermentation
Fermentation allows glycolysis to continue by regenerating NAD⁺ from NADH.
The key idea is:
NADH→NAD+ 
This permits glycolysis to continue temporarily.
But fermentation produces much less ATP.
Primarily:
2ATP/glucose 
from glycolysis.

28. Waterlogging and Respiration
This is extremely important in agriculture.
When soil becomes waterlogged:
Waterlogging→Soil pore spaces filled with water→O2 diffusion decreases 
  O 2 
Roots can experience hypoxia or anoxia.
Then:
Root aerobic respiration↓   
and plants may increase fermentative metabolism.

29. Hypoxia vs Anoxia
Hypoxia
Oxygen concentration is lower than normal.

Anoxia
Very little or essentially no oxygen is available.
Therefore:
Hypoxia=low O₂ =   
Anoxia=extremely low/absent O₂ =   

30. Respiration in Seeds
Respiration is particularly important during germination.
A dry seed has relatively low metabolic activity.
After imbibition:
Water uptake→Metabolic activation→Respiration increases    Respiration provides ATP required for:
- Enzyme synthesis
- Cell division
- Cell expansion
- Mobilization of food reserves
- Root emergence
- Shoot development

31. Seed Germination and Respiration
The sequence can be simplified as:
Dry seed→Imbibition→Respiratory activation→Reserve mobilization→Seedling growth       

This is why adequate oxygen is important for normal seed germination.

32. Respiration in Roots
Roots require ATP for:
- Ion uptake
- Nutrient absorption
- Root growth
- Membrane transport
- Root metabolism
- Maintenance
Therefore:
Healthy roots→Active respiration→Energy for nutrient uptake     

33. Respiration and Nutrient Uptake
Many mineral nutrients are taken up through processes requiring metabolic energy.
For example, active transport depends on ATP-driven ion gradients.
Therefore:
Respiration→ATP→Active transport→Nutrient acquisition   ATP
   
Poor root aeration can therefore reduce nutrient uptake.

34. Respiration and Growth
Growth requires:
- Cell division
- Cell expansion
- Protein synthesis
- Cell-wall synthesis

- Membrane formation
All require metabolic energy.
Thus:
Respiration→ATP→Growth   ATP   

35. Respiration and Plant Development
Respiration is required during:
- Germination
- Seedling establishment
- Vegetative growth
- Flowering
- Fruit development
- Seed filling
- Maturation
- Senescence
It is therefore a continuous component of plant metabolism.

36. Respiration and Photosynthesis
The relationship is especially important for crop productivity.
Photosynthesis:
CO2→Organic carbonCO 2  Respiration:
Organic carbon→CO2+usable energy   CO 2+ 
Therefore:
Photosynthesis = carbon gain   
Respiration = energy release + carbon expenditure   

37. Gross Photosynthesis vs Net Photosynthesis
Suppose a plant fixes:
100100
units of carbon through photosynthesis.
If respiration consumes:
units, the simplified net carbon gain would be:
100−30=70100-30=70
Thus:
Net carbon gain=Gross carbon fixation−Respiratory carbon loss  =
  -    
Actual plant carbon balance also includes other carbon losses and processes.

38. Respiration and Temperature
Respiration is strongly influenced by temperature.
Within a suitable physiological range:
Temperature↑→Respiration rate↑    But excessive temperature can damage cellular systems and eventually reduce respiration.
Therefore, the relationship is not indefinitely linear.

39. High Temperature and Crop Productivity
High temperatures can increase respiratory carbon loss.
Conceptually:
High temperature→Respiration↑    while simultaneously:
Heat stress→Photosynthesis↓    This can reduce net carbon accumulation.

Therefore:
Heat stress can reduce crop productivity through both sides of the carbon balance   

40. Respiration and Water Stress
Severe drought can alter respiration.
Effects may include:
- Reduced substrate supply
- Altered mitochondrial activity
- Oxidative stress
- Changes in root metabolism
- Changes in maintenance costs
The exact response depends on:
- Crop
- Tissue
- Severity
- Duration
- Temperature
- Developmental stage

41. Respiration and Oxygen Availability
Aerobic respiration depends on adequate oxygen.
Therefore:
O2↓→Aerobic respiration capacity↓O 2   When oxygen becomes severely limiting:
Fermentation↑  This can occur in:
- Waterlogged roots

- Flooded tissues
- Poorly aerated soils
- Compact soils
- Germinating seeds in poorly aerated conditions

42. Soil Compaction and Respiration
Compacted soil can reduce air-filled pore space.
Therefore:
Soil compaction→Poor aeration→O2 availability↓→Root respiration stress    O 2    
This is one reason good soil structure is important.

43. Respiration in Different Plant Organs
Respiration occurs in living tissues including:

Leaves
Provides energy for:
- Maintenance
- Transport
- Metabolism

Roots
Provides energy for:
- Nutrient uptake
- Growth
- Maintenance

Stems
Supports:
- Growth

- Transport
- Maintenance

Flowers
Supports:
- Development
- Reproductive processes

Seeds
Supports:
- Germination
- Development

Fruits
Supports:
- Growth
- Maturation
- Ripening
- Maintenance

44. Respiration in Fruits
Respiration continues after harvest.
This is extremely important in post-harvest agriculture.
After harvest:
Fruit respiration→Stored carbohydrate consumption    
As respiration continues:
- Stored substrates decrease
- CO₂ is released
- Heat is produced
- Senescence eventually progresses

45. Climacteric Fruits
Some fruits show a characteristic rise in respiration during ripening.
Examples include:
- Banana
- Mango
- Apple
- Papaya
- Tomato
This is associated with the climacteric pattern.

46. Non-Climacteric Fruits
Other fruits do not show the same characteristic climacteric respiratory rise during ripening.
Examples commonly include:
- Grape
- Citrus
- Strawberry
The physiology of fruit ripening is complex, so climacteric/non-climacteric is a useful classification
rather than a complete description of all ripening behavior.

47. Respiration and Ethylene
In many climacteric fruits:
Ethylene↔Ripening↔Respiratory changes 
Ethylene can regulate:
- Ripening
- Softening

- Color change
- Aroma development
- Enzyme activity

48. Post-Harvest Importance
Higher respiration generally means faster depletion of stored reserves.
Therefore:
Higher respiration rate→Faster deterioration    
This is why post-harvest technologies often aim to slow respiration.
Methods include:
- Refrigeration
- Appropriate temperature control
- Modified atmosphere packaging
- Controlled atmosphere storage
- Humidity management
- Proper ventilation
- Ethylene management

49. Respiration and Storage
Stored agricultural products continue to respire.
Examples:
- Seeds
- Grains
- Fruits
- Vegetables
- Tubers
If storage conditions are poor:

Respiration↑→Heat↑→Moisture problems→Quality loss      High respiration can also favor deterioration and microbial growth when moisture and temperature are
suitable.

50. Respiration in Stored Grains
Grains are living tissues.
Under proper storage:
Respiration↓  If grain moisture becomes too high:
Respiration↑  This can lead to:
- Heating
- Dry-matter loss
- Quality deterioration
- Increased microbial activity
- Reduced seed viability
Therefore proper moisture and temperature management are critical.

51. Respiratory Quotient
The Respiratory Quotient (RQ) is:
RQ=CO₂ producedO₂ consumed        
It gives information about the type of respiratory substrate being metabolized.

52. RQ for Carbohydrates
For glucose:
C6H12O6+6O2→6CO2+6H2OC 6H  12 O 6+6O 2  6CO 2+6H 2O

Therefore:
RQ=66=1RQ=  6 =1
So:
RQcarbohydrate≈1   1  

53. RQ for Fats
Fat oxidation generally requires more oxygen relative to the CO₂ produced.
Therefore:
RQfat<1  <1  
A typical approximate value is around:
0.70.7
depending on the specific substrate.

54. RQ for Organic Acids
Organic acids can have:
RQ>1 
because their oxidation may produce relatively more CO₂ compared with oxygen consumption.
Therefore RQ can provide clues about respiratory substrates.

55. Factors Affecting Respiration Rate
Important factors include:

Temperature
Generally increases respiration within a physiological range.

Oxygen
Required for aerobic respiration.

Water
Metabolism generally requires adequate hydration.

Substrate availability
More available respiratory substrate can influence respiration.

Tissue age
Young, actively growing tissues often have high metabolic activity.

Tissue type
Meristems, fruits, seeds and actively growing tissues can have different rates.

Developmental stage
Respiration changes throughout development.

Stress
Heat, drought, flooding, salinity and injury can alter respiration.

56. Respiration and Plant Age
Respiration rate generally varies with developmental stage.
Young tissues often have high metabolic demands because they are actively:
- Dividing
- Expanding
- Synthesizing proteins
- Building cell walls
Mature tissues often have different maintenance requirements.
Senescing tissues undergo substantial metabolic changes.

57. Maintenance Respiration vs Growth
Respiration
Plant respiration can conceptually be divided into:

Growth respiration
Energy required to produce new biomass.
Growth respiration→Construction of new tissue    

Maintenance respiration
Energy required to keep existing tissues alive and functioning.
Examples:
- Membrane maintenance
- Ion gradients
- Protein turnover
- Cellular repair
Maintenance respiration→Keeping existing tissue functional 
  

58. Growth Respiration
Growing organs require energy for:
- Cell division
- Cell expansion
- Protein synthesis
- Nucleic acid synthesis
- Cell-wall formation
- Membrane production
Therefore:
Growth→High biosynthetic demand→Respiration     

59. Maintenance Respiration
Even when a plant is not visibly growing, it remains metabolically active.
Cells must maintain:

- Membrane integrity
- Ion gradients
- Protein turnover
- Repair
- Cellular organization
This requires ATP.
Thus:
No visible growth≠No respiration   

60. Respiration and Crop Yield
Crop yield depends partly on the balance between carbon fixation and carbon expenditure.
Conceptually:
Photosynthetic carbon gain−Respiratory carbon loss→available carbon  -     
Then:
Available carbon→Growth + Storage + Reproduction    
Therefore respiration is essential, but excessive respiratory carbon loss under unfavorable conditions
can reduce net biomass accumulation.

61. Respiration and Source–Sink Relationships
Photosynthesis creates assimilates in source tissues.
Respiration provides energy to both sources and sinks.
For example:
Leaf→Sucrose→Seed    The developing seed uses respiration to convert substrates into energy and biomass.
Therefore:
Source supplies carbon+Respiration supplies usable energy  +
   

62. Respiration During Seed Filling
Developing seeds require substantial metabolic activity.
Respiration supports:
- Protein synthesis
- Oil synthesis
- Starch synthesis
- Cell division
- Embryo development
Thus:
Photosynthesis→Assimilate supply   and:
Respiration→Energy for seed development   63. Respiration and Oilseeds
Oil-rich seeds such as:
- Soybean
- Groundnut
- Sunflower
- Mustard
- Sesame
convert assimilates into storage lipids.
This requires metabolic energy.
Therefore respiration supports biosynthesis of storage reserves.

64. Respiration and Legumes
Legume roots form associations with nitrogen-fixing bacteria.
Nitrogen fixation requires considerable energy.

Therefore root respiration supports the energy requirements associated with:
- Nodulation
- Nitrogen fixation
- Transport
- Assimilation of nitrogen
Thus:
Root respiration→ATP→Energy-demanding nitrogen metabolism 
 ATP   

65. Respiration and Stress
Respiration changes under:
- Heat
- Drought
- Flooding
- Salinity
- Nutrient deficiency
- Pest damage
- Disease
- Wounding
However, the direction and magnitude of the response depend on:
- Plant species
- Tissue
- Stress intensity
- Stress duration
- Developmental stage
- Acclimation

66. Oxidative Stress
Respiration involves electron transfer.
Under some stressful conditions, cells can generate reactive oxygen species (ROS).
Examples include:
- Superoxide
- Hydrogen peroxide
Plants possess antioxidant systems to control ROS.
Important antioxidants include:
- Superoxide dismutase
- Catalase
- Ascorbate peroxidase
- Glutathione-related systems
A moderate amount of ROS also functions in signaling, while excessive accumulation can cause
- xidative damage.

67. Alternative Oxidase
Plants possess a distinctive respiratory pathway involving alternative oxidase (AOX).
Instead of transferring all electrons through the standard cytochrome pathway, electrons can be diverted
through the alternative pathway.
This generally:
- Reduces proton-pumping efficiency
- Produces less ATP per electron pair
- Can help maintain electron flow under certain metabolic conditions
- Plays roles in plant metabolic flexibility and stress responses
This is one important difference between plant respiration and simplified textbook descriptions of
animal/eukaryotic respiration.

68. Plant Respiration Is Metabolically Flexible
Respiration is not simply:
Glucose→CO2+H2O  CO 2+H 2O
Plants can use many substrates.
These include:
- Sugars
- Starch-derived sugars
- Organic acids
- Lipids
- Amino acids
Respiratory intermediates also provide building blocks for biosynthesis.
Therefore:
Respiration=Energy production+Metabolic integration =  +   

69. Respiration as a Central Metabolic Hub
Respiration connects with:
- Carbohydrate metabolism
- Lipid metabolism
- Protein metabolism
- Nitrogen metabolism
- Organic acid metabolism
- Growth
- Stress responses
For example:
Carbohydrates→Glycolysis   Lipids→Fattyacid breakdown→Acetyl-CoA    Proteins→Amino acids→Respiratory intermediates    70. Photosynthesis–Respiration–Transpiration
Connection
These three processes are tightly connected.

Photosynthesis
Captures carbon:
CO2→organic carbonCO 2 Respiration
Releases usable energy:
- rganic carbon→ATP  ATP

Transpiration
Regulates water movement and helps create the water potential gradient through the plant.
Together:
Photosynthesis+Respiration+Transpiration=Core plant physiological processes  +   +   =    

71. Day and Night Relationship
Day
Light
↓
Photosynthesis
↓
Sugar production
↓
Respiration
↓
ATP + cellular metabolism

Night
Stored sugars/starch
↓
Respiration
↓
ATP
↓
Cellular maintenance + growth

Therefore plants remain metabolically active at night.

72. Respiration and Crop Management
Farmers indirectly influence plant respiration through:

Irrigation management
Avoids severe drought and waterlogging.

Soil aeration
Maintains oxygen availability to roots.

Soil structure
Good structure supports gas exchange.

Temperature management
Reduces excessive heat stress.

Nutrient management
Supports balanced growth and metabolism.

Pest and disease management
Reduces tissue damage and stress-induced metabolic costs.

Storage management
Reduces unnecessary post-harvest respiration.

73. Agricultural Importance of Respiration
Respiration is important for:

Seed germination
Provides energy for embryo growth.

Root development
Provides ATP for growth and nutrient uptake.

Vegetative growth
Supports synthesis of new tissues.

Flowering
Supports reproductive development.

Fruit development
Provides energy for cell division and expansion.

Seed filling
Supports synthesis of starch, proteins and oils.

Stress tolerance
Supports repair and defense.

Post-harvest storage
Respiration determines how rapidly stored products consume reserves.

78. Respiration in the Complete Crop Life Cycle
Respiration is involved throughout the crop life cycle:
Seed→Germination→Seedling→Vegetative growth→Flowering→Fruit/seed formation→Maturity→S
enescence          
At every stage:

Respiration→ATP→Metabolic activity   ATP   

79. The Most Important Agricultural Concept
Crop productivity depends on the balance between carbon acquisition and carbon expenditure.
A simplified model is:
Photosynthetic carbon gain−Respiratory carbon loss=Net carbon available  -   =    
Then:
Net carbon available→Growth + Storage + Reproduction 
  
This is why respiration is neither "bad" nor something plants should eliminate.
Plants need respiration to live.
The agricultural objective is not zero respiration; it is maintaining efficient metabolism and avoiding
unnecessarily high respiratory losses caused by unfavorable conditions such as excessive heat, injury or
poor storage.

80. Final One-Read Summary
Remember respiration as:
Food→Glycolysis→Pyruvate→Acetyl-CoA→Krebs Cycle→NADH/
FADH2→ETC→ATP        2
   
The essential concepts are:
1. Respiration occurs continuously in living plant cells.
2. Aerobic respiration mainly occurs through glycolysis, pyruvate oxidation, the Krebs cycle
and oxidative phosphorylation.
3. Glycolysis occurs in the cytosol.
4. The Krebs cycle occurs mainly in the mitochondrial matrix.
5. The electron transport chain is located in the inner mitochondrial membrane.
6. Oxygen is the terminal electron acceptor in aerobic respiration.

7. ATP provides usable energy for cellular activities.
8. Plants also respire at night.
9. Waterlogging can restrict oxygen availability and force tissues toward fermentation.
10.Respiration supports germination, growth, nutrient uptake, flowering, fruiting and seed
filling.
11.Respiration continues after harvest in fruits, vegetables, seeds and grains.
12.Excessive respiration after harvest accelerates depletion of stored reserves and
deterioration.
13.Temperature, oxygen, water, substrate availability, tissue age and stress influence
respiration.
14.Photosynthesis captures carbon, while respiration makes chemical energy available and
returns some carbon as CO₂.
The central relationship is:
Photosynthesis→Organic matter→Respiration→ATP→Growth, maintenance and reproduction       
So, in crop science:
Photosynthesis provides the carbon; respiration provides the usable energy.
   

Transpiration in Plants
## 1. Introduction
Transpiration is the process by which plants lose water in the form of water vapor, mainly from their
aerial parts, especially through tiny pores called stomata in leaves.
A simple representation is:
Soil water→Roots→Xylem→Leaves→Atmosphere       
At the leaf:
H2Oliquid→H2Ovapor→Atmosphere    H 2O       
Transpiration is not simply "water loss." It is an important component of the plant's water relations,
mineral transport, cooling, stomatal regulation, and photosynthesis.

## 2. Definition
Transpiration is the loss of water from a living plant, primarily in the form of water
vapor, from aerial plant surfaces.
The majority of transpiration in many crops occurs through stomata.
Other routes are:
1. Stomatal transpiration
2. Cuticular transpiration
3. Lenticular transpiration

3. Why Is Transpiration Important?
Transpiration contributes to:
- Movement of water through the plant
- Transport of dissolved minerals
- Cooling of leaves
- Maintenance of plant water relations
- Generation of transpiration pull
- Xylem flow
- Regulation of stomata
- Coordination between water uptake and CO₂ uptake
- Photosynthetic activity
- Nutrient acquisition
The overall relationship is:
Root water uptake→Xylem transport→Leaf water loss     

4. Transpiration Is Different from Evaporation
These terms are related but not identical.

Evaporation
Water changes from liquid to vapor from a surface.

Transpiration
Water vapor is lost from plant tissues, primarily through stomata.
Thus:
Transpiration = biological water loss   
while evaporation can occur from:
- Soil
- Water surfaces
- Wet leaves
- Irrigation water

5. Transpiration vs Guttation
These are frequently confused.
Feature
Water form
Main route
Main driving
force
Usually occurs
Water contains
Visible?
Therefore:

Transpiration

Guttation

Vapor

Hydathodes

Common during active atmospheric
drying
